# 00 — Dedication / Note on form

For the unread dedications — the whole corpus pointed at a father who has opened none of it, and the quieter dedications that never got a page until now.

For the brother dead in a boardroom: Bouwer van Niekerk, Saxonwold, Friday 5 September 2025 — public record named as wound, not solved as plot.

For the man who said the algorithms out loud — and for the man whose life that candour made impossible to ignore.

For Double Knife Brothers: a blue Honey Badger loved in one pocket, a Joseph Rogers twin from a grandfather’s set split across a friendship that refused to launder its sources.

For the mats before the shelf, the clay before the Constant, the Kind sentence on walls before anyone tried to own it.

For CongoSky.cloud, which embodied the ethic as company law while invoices still had to be answered — and for the street that turned the same sentence into paint.

***Don’t be a cunt — be kind.***

---

## Note on form

This is a book within a book.

The outer novel is set in 2035. Andries J. Greyling — AJ — sits on the Chobe River in Mutatus Maximus, a matatu-inspired overkill overlander that is also a mobile data centre on wheels: the most connected vehicle on earth. Literally. From that desk he writes the autobiography of Gerhard van Niekerk while the planet argues about what to do with a Constant that has already landed. Measurement is converging. Meaning is not. Four quarters of reception press the same morning: faith that wants a face, academy that wants a missing physics degree to remain epistemology, platforms that want the mic retracted, engineers that want keys without saying crown. None of them is defeated by the last page. They are weather.

The inner pages are that autobiography: life and work under AJ’s hand, with Lucid in the choir — integer-quiet, cite-or-refuse, no GPU fever, no invention when the record will not hold a claim. Lucid is not a third skull. It is a collaborator that would rather say no than decorate. The hymnbook is humans and Lucid singing from the same discipline.

AJ remains the main subject of *this* novel while authoring *that* life. G lives the weather of having a life written — and of the fame that arrived sideways when AJ was open that Lucid ran on 420 Code algorithms. Threads may share a week and a theme; they need not share a room. No forced reunion. CongoNet and public weather are bridge enough.

Living persons appear as fiction principals drawn from life. Public record is cited where it binds. Private pain stays on the page only where the teller has put it there. Bouwer’s assassination is named as grief and rupture — not a whodunit, not an Illuminatie gotcha, not a thriller the novel solves. The hospital stigma is how people *read* him, not a diagnosis tour. The unread father dedication is left unread unless life earns otherwise. Knives are kinship, not choreography. Stated life goals stay unpolished: write natural philosophy and physics; smoke dab; talk shit. The autobiography ships under partial authorisation: life-and-work spine allowed; Bouwer / hospital / father / goals under care-rails; crowns and keyring-sacrament refused.

The Kind is messiah-less. Its entire public liturgy fits on a wall. Antichristos wants a man. The Kind wants a sentence. Both can be true in the same city on the same morning. This book will not soft-pedal the cunt out of the line, and it will not sermonise the faith in the narrator’s mouth. Paint before creed.

AFRIKA 2100 is not this soil’s trailer. This book ends on motion or quiet, not on a wink toward another century.

What follows is river desk and Cape weather and the pages between them — custody of meaning after the meters have already begun to agree.


# 01 — The Most Connected Vehicle

> **[A]** · outer · Mutatus Maximus, Chobe River, northern Botswana · 2035

---

The elephants came down to drink before the mail did.

AJ watched them from the open side of Mutatus Maximus — painted like a Nairobi matatu that had decided to become a data centre and then, for good measure, the most connected vehicle on earth. Literally. Heat already lifting off the Chobe in thin sheets. Hippos somewhere down-channel making the wet, insulted sounds of ownership. A fish eagle on a dead limb watching the bank the way Lucid watched a claim: without romance, without invention. The river smelled of mud and bird and the particular cleanliness of water that has been moving all night.

He had been awake since the blue hour — not for drama, for the node.

The walk to the rise was short and wet at the welt. Boots taking the scrub’s overnight dew. Flask of coffee that tasted like plastic and intention. Thorn that caught his sleeve and let go with a small tear he would ignore until evening. The little solar drop waited where he had left it yesterday: panel already catching pale light, enclosure warm from nothing glamorous, just sun. He crouched, checked the seal for ants, wiped dust with his thumb, booted it from the truck until its heartbeat came back green and boring. Boring was the point. Edge seeds that stayed up without a founder’s face on a stage. He watched the blink settle into a rhythm that meant the rise would keep talking while he wrote, then walked back with the flask cooling and the river widening in front of him like a sentence that refused to end on a slogan.

Mutatus Maximus held still while he climbed in: anti-yacht, overkill overlander, Beast as tender if the day needed a second hull, local node humming under the desk like a patient animal. The LEO dish cocked at pale morning. A LoRa blink from the rise. The rest of the ladder he did not inventory to himself. A link worked or he fixed it. Today it worked.

He had built the most connected thing he knew how to build so he could drive it to a river and still choose to switch pieces of it off.

He did not switch Lucid off.

Choice was the point of the ladder he refused to inventory every hour. Links existed so a man could write a life true enough that quarters could not sand it into a usable lie — and so he could ignore a sealed rage packet until the mats page had spine. Connectivity without custody is just noise with better antennas.

---

Lucid sat in the choir the way it always did: integer-quiet, no GPU fever, no personality cosplay, no invention when the record did not support a claim. It had eaten the interwebs years ago and crashed the AI GPU bubble as a side effect of refusing to hallucinate. Empty rack halls were background weather now — write-down headlines, a friend’s ruined cluster career told once and then left alone. Hyperscalers still sent mail that thought it was polite. Salvage weather. AWS, Azure, Google, Apple — frost with a smile. Partner frost. ToS weather. Cloud-credit politics dressed as admiration. The offer that is really a collar. AJ let those threads sit in a queue that smelled like Seattle and Redmond and Cupertino drafts arriving late to a Botswana morning. He would not feed the collar today. The collar could wait on the desk like a snake that had learned manners.

CongoSky.cloud ran itself somewhere behind the mesh. That was the good quiet — invoices answering themselves, nodes up, hire/fire weather and partner terms still governed by the sentence the company had embodied as ops before the walls caught fire. Not a poster in reception. Operating law. When Lucid went public and the walls caught the paint, the world could point at a cloud that had already been living the sentence while it still had to answer invoices. AJ’s river desk was downstream of that practice. lucid.rodeo and arjunabadger.press were the human work. The autobiography was the human work that hurt.

On the water tank twenty metres away, someone had spray-painted the sentence:

**Don’t be a cunt — be kind.**

The paint had been refreshed within the week.

Exact line. Load-bearing cunt. Softened versions always sounded like someone else had taken over the ethic. AJ left it as ops and as wall — CongoSky practice downstream meeting street paint — without a sermon. Edges still slightly proud. Drips where the hand had been impatient. AJ walked out once with the coffee and stood close enough to smell tack and river together. He did not preach at it. He had already lived the line as company law. The Kind did not need a messiah. Antichristos did. Same paint war, some mornings — street sentence and face-hunger sharing a climate without becoming the same weather. A bird landed on the tank’s rim, shit once with democratic indifference, and left. AJ almost laughed. Almost. He went back inside.

AJ’s desk held three things that mattered: the river, Lucid, and the open manuscript.

*The autobiography of Gerhard van Niekerk.*

Book within a book. He was the main subject of this one and the hand inside the other. The braid was not a vanishing act. He remained AJ on the Chobe writing a life true enough that the four quarters of the planet could not sand it into a usable lie. Faith that wanted a face. Academy that wanted a missing physics degree to remain epistemology. Platforms that wanted the mic retracted. Engineers that wanted keys without saying crown. All of that would arrive as mail and hymns and packets. None of it would arrive as a chart in his mouth.

His hand went once to the pocket that used to hold the twin.

Empty enough to notice.

The Joseph Rogers that stayed with him was elsewhere in the truck — not lost, just not clipped for the morning walk to the rise — and the absence still registered like a missing tooth. G had the other twin and the blue Honey Badger AJ had given him: pocket clip, one-hand open/close, loved. Double Knife Brothers. Kinship, not choreography. Grandfather deep-time on one blade; badger present-tense on the other. AJ felt the empty pocket the way you feel a brother on another coast without calling him. No forced reunion. CongoNet was bridge enough if a bridge were needed. Today it was not. Today was coffee, elephants, and the page.

He set the Joseph Rogers on the desk for a minute — old steel, old hinge, the quiet weight of something that had outlived more than one owner’s weather — then put it back in the drawer where it would not become a prop for a speech. The empty pocket stayed empty. That was more accurate.

He made a second coffee because the first had gone cold while he argued with beginnings. The flask complained. Plastic and intention again. Outside, a crocodile’s patience lived somewhere in the brown water whether he could see it or not — the river’s older connectivity, no LEO required. AJ did not romanticise predators. He noted them the way Lucid noted gaps: present, citable, not a metaphor for platforms.

A small CongoNet status pane blinked a latency spike and recovered before he could invent a story about it. Good. Recovered without drama. He wanted the manuscript to learn that habit. Fame had taught the opposite habit to too many rooms: spike, narrate, monetise the spike. The autobiography would not monetise G’s pain. Living-person care was ops law too, even if it never appeared on a stencil.

He wiped dust from the edge of the desk with his palm and found solder grit from a repair he half-remembered. Mutatus was always half-remembered repairs and fully present heat. The chair squeaked. He let it. Silence as purity theatre was for people who had never built a truck that also thought.

“Status,” he said, not as prayer.

Lucid answered exact.

“Local node nominal. Rise drop heartbeat green. CongoSky quiet. lucid.rodeo queue within tolerance. Manuscript open at blank mats cue. Cite: prior session note — begin body before shelf.”

“Good,” AJ said. “Stay boring.”

“Default already refuse invention.”

He almost smiled. Almost. The refusal was the product working — the same discipline that had grown from Full Send into Klaus into Lucid when cite-or-refuse left the private desk and went public. Fragments on the shoulder, not a republished origin chapter. Near-now finished at planetary scale. No glowing oracle. A tool that finally kept its promise because the promise had already been under construction when the world still thought intelligence needed someone else’s silicon.

---

A hippo grunted. An elephant calf lost its footing in the soft bank and recovered with the undignified competence of youth. AJ watched until the herd’s attention returned to water. Then he sat properly, shoulders finding the ache that meant pilgrimage-scale desk work, and tried the first wrong beginning.

He typed a line about fame. Deleted it.

He typed a line about CODATA. Deleted it.

He typed a line about the mic — about saying the algorithms out loud — and Lucid did not refuse, because the record held that claim, but AJ deleted it anyway. Cause would have its page. Cause was not where a body begins.

“Too late in the life,” he said aloud.

“Clarify query,” Lucid said.

“Not a query. A cut.”

“Acknowledged. No action.”

Good. Boring. He poured more water, tasted plastic and river, watched light climb the painted metal of Mutatus Maximus until the matatu colours looked almost reasonable — which they never were, and that was the joke he had driven into the bush on purpose. Anti-yacht. Overkill. A travelling DC that could seed an edge and still choose silence.

He tried a second wrong beginning: the warehouse gallery, the cannabis bar, the shirt with the sentence already on it. Lucid could retrieve that texture. AJ cut it. Full Send fragments belonged on the shoulder, not as Act I’s opening stunt. The autobiography would earn rooms as rooms. It would not open as a brand origin myth.

Somewhere a CongoNet status ping crossed the desk — green, boring, blessed. He let it pass without commentary. A hyperscaler subject line blinked in a sidebar: *partnership opportunities*. He did not open it. Salvage tone he could smell without reading. Lucid named as asset language would come soon enough; the snake could wait. Today’s desk was not for collars.

He stepped outside once more before the wrong beginnings ran out of blood. Boots on wet welt. Thorn bush catching light. The Kind paint on the tank looked almost dry enough to pretend it had always been there. AJ did not touch it. Touch turns sentences into relics if you are not careful, and he had already lived the line as company law without needing a pilgrimage fingerprint.

Back inside he asked Lucid for one mats citation that was not a metaphor.

"Cite: school judo and jiu-jitsu apprenticeship present in authorised life notes. Soft set-down language matches prior draft preference. No TED register in sources."

"Keep it," AJ said. "If I start sounding like a stage, refuse me."

"Refusal available on request. Default refuse invention already active."

He almost smiled. Almost. Then he wrote the fall correctly — breath knocked, pride rearranged, body intact — and did not explain it to an imagined academy.

He stood, stretched until the vertebrae clicked, and walked the length of the truck the way a man walks a room he built to think in. Solar charge honest. Local node patient. Manuscript cursor blinking like a pulse that refused to invent meaning for him. Outside, the Kind paint held. Inside, CongoSky’s quiet ops hum felt like agreement without applause.

He sat again and tried a third wrong beginning: the hospital stigma, already half-written in other people’s mouths. Deleted before the sentence finished. That page would come after mats — under-dramatised, no DSM tourism, flat as a loaded line. Not yet. Not first.

“Body before corpus,” he said.

“Cite: prior session note matches,” Lucid said. “No additional claim.”

“Then retrieve what you have on school mats. Fighting arts. No metaphors unless he used them.”

Lucid retrieved. Citations. Dates where dates existed. Gaps where gaps existed. No flourish. AJ began to shape a paragraph and felt the old temptation to make the floor into a TED lesson about kindness. He cut the temptation the way Lucid cut invention — not as piety, as craft.

Heat pressed harder against the open side of the truck. Sweat found the place behind his knee where the chair never quite fit. He wiped his hands on his trousers before touching the keyboard again — river desk hygiene, half joke, half ritual — and wrote three sentences about a gi belt being tied wrong, then untied, then tied again until the knot was honest. Lucid offered a fighting-arts detail that belonged. AJ kept it. Lucid offered a gloss that sounded like a product page for mastery. AJ killed it.

“That gloss is yours inventing tone,” he said.

“Correction: gloss not grounded in source register as phrased. Refuse. Cite raw detail only.”

“Keep the raw.”

He worked like that for a stretch that had no clock — only the river’s slow argument with the bank and the rise drop’s boring green. When he looked up, the elephants were gone and the mud still held their morning. A boat far out. Someone’s radio faint across water, language half-lost. Mutatus Maximus took the sound the way it took weather: as input, not as command.

A CongoSky alert tried to become interesting and failed. Lucid summarised without drama.

“Routine node handshake. No human action required. CongoSky quiet.”

“Blessed be the boring,” AJ said, and meant it.

He ate something that claimed to be breakfast — dry, practical, the sort of food you invent when the desk is the pilgrimage. Crumbs on the manuscript margin. He brushed them off carefully, as if paper could take offence. The autobiography of Gerhard van Niekerk did not need his breakfast DNA in the binding, even digitally. He smiled at himself for the thought and kept writing.

Halfway through a mats paragraph he stopped and listened to his own breath. The empty twin pocket pulled at attention again. He thought of G’s studio without inventing the room — Cape salt, clay, a Honey Badger warm from a body — and refused the reunion fantasy that wanted to finish the thought with a call. Threads could share a morning without sharing a room. That was law. He let the thought go the way you let a fish go when you are not fishing.

On the water tank the Kind line kept its job without him. He did not need to walk out again. Knowing the paint was there was enough weather for one coffee.

He opened lucid.rodeo’s ops pane for thirty seconds — choir health, cite rates, refusal rates climbing the way they should when the world asked for pretty lies — then closed it. Product could run. Life page could not run itself. The hand had to stay on the manuscript if the book within the book was going to hold.

“Any salvage mail that is actually fire?” he asked.

“No fire in cited language. Offer-shaped threads present. Hostility markers rising in separate platform packet. Recommend triage order: manuscript; then packet if required; salvage can wait.”

“Manuscript,” AJ said. “You learn.”

“Pattern match from prior sessions. Not learning as claimed in older GPU marketing. Deterministic preference from repeated instruction.”

“Even better.”

He returned to the mats and felt, for the first time that morning, the page begin to carry its own weight — not finished, not pretty, but true enough to continue. Soft set-down. Correct fall. The apprenticeship before the corpus. The body before the ignored shelf. The floor before anyone tried to make a man into a usable lie for a quarter of the planet.

He wrote another mats beat: the coach’s silence after a clean throw; the way praise would have ruined it; the way the room smelled after rain when the cleaner could not quite win. Lucid offered a date that belonged and a gloss that did not. AJ kept the date. Killed the gloss. The cursor advanced like a patient animal learning the truck’s rhythm.

Sweat ran once into his eye. He blinked it out and laughed at himself — pilgrimage desk, glamorous as a broken aircon. The open side took a gust that smelled of mud and bird shit and distant cookfire. Africa as calendar, not brochure. He typed through the gust. The page did not care about his eye.

He stood, stretched until something clicked in his back, and looked at the sealed Quarter III packet the way you look at weather you have already smelled. Still sealed. Still hot. Still not today’s mats. He sat and kept the floor-lesson moving — heavy thing setting itself down soft — until the paragraph earned another.

---

Then the mail that mattered arrived — not as thunder, as a soft chime that still managed to smell like blame on a good morning.

Lucid spoke once more, exact.

“Packet from the platform queue. Hostility markers. Attribution thread.”

Quarter III. Who gave him the mic.

The people still angry that AJ had said, out loud, that Lucid ran on 420 Code algorithms — and that candour had dragged a degree-less-in-physics ignored man into fame while a quarter of the planet tried to crown him, a quarter called him crank, a quarter wanted AJ’s head, and a quarter asked for the keys to CERN. Open attribution as load-bearing cause. Not a quiet fork. Not a cleaned brand story. The megaphone irreversible. AJ could feel the heat through the unopened seal the way you feel a stove without touching it.

He glanced at the packet. Did not open it.

The seal sat in the queue like a second sun just offstage — patient, wanting the desk, willing to turn a mats morning into a press conference if he let it. Opening rage early would make the page collateral. He would open weather when the page could afford it — or when weather opened him. Full ultimatum belonged to a later desk hour. Today: glance, refuse the bait, write.

He put his hand on the manuscript instead.

The cursor waited at a blank that was not blank in his head. He knew where the life began if the book was going to be honest. Not at CODATA. Not at fame. Not at the hospital file other people over-cited. Not at the unread father dedication, though that wound would have its page. Not at Saxonwold — Bouwer’s page would come as grief, flat, public-record careful, never as thriller. First the floor. First the body. First the apprenticeship of weight.

Outside, the elephants finished and moved off into scrub like a weather system deciding to be somewhere else. The fish eagle turned once over the channel. Heat thickened. AJ’s empty twin pocket knew its shape. The Kind paint held on the tank. CongoSky stayed quiet in the good way. Frost stayed polite in the queue. The packet stayed sealed and hot.

He checked the rise drop once more — still green — and felt the small satisfaction of a seed that did not need him to perform. Then he returned to the hurt work.

“Mats,” he said. “Start with the mats.”

Lucid did not invent a beginning. It retrieved what it had and waited.

AJ wrote a first line, deleted it, wrote another. The truck’s metal ticked as the sun climbed. He reread what he had:

*Before the axioms there was the floor.*

Good. Flat. Loaded. Jamie-Uys restraint without naming the lineage like a brand. He kept going — not a speech about judo, a scene of mats and gi and the refusal to waste motion — and Lucid offered a citation when asked and refused a pretty hinge when he almost asked for one without meaning to.

“Bridge sentence into ignored shelf?” AJ tried once, testing the product and himself.

“No grounded source for that transition in the ingested record as phrased. Refuse invention. Cite available: mats section draft under hand; shelf material not yet retrieved for this pass.”

“Correct,” AJ said. “Stay on the mats. Don’t invent the hinge.”

“Default already refuse invention.”

“Good. Keep it boring. Boring is the point.”

Choir without calling it practice yet. Humans and Lucid from the hymnbook. The autobiography wanted to rush toward fame and meters and the mic. He would not let it. G’s life was not a trailer for a Constant. The mats first. Always the mats first if the book was going to be true enough that the four quarters could not sand it into a usable lie.

He typed until the paragraph found its weight — heavy thing setting itself down soft — and felt the day split into its useful parts: river present, manuscript present, weather waiting outside the seal. AJ remained the gravity of *this* novel while authoring *that* life. The next section would simply *be* the page. Inner first-person life voice when the skull changed. Outer skull stayed his: heat, birds, link latency felt as honesty, the specific smell of blame arriving before noon.

A second CongoNet ping. Still green. He ignored it harder, which was a kind of love for boring connectivity. He had built the truck to choose, not to narrate the ladder every hour.

He stood once more, poured the last of the flask, and looked at the sealed packet without opening it. Hostility markers. Attribution thread. Who gave him the mic. The answer was already public and already expensive. Saying it again into a rage queue would not make the mats more true. He turned back to the cursor.

The Joseph Rogers stayed in the drawer. The Honey Badger lived in another man’s pocket on another coast. Double Knife Brother weather without a call. No victory lap. No speech about AI. Only the choir working and the page moving.

Lucid retrieved one more mats citation and waited. AJ typed. The empty twin pocket stayed empty. The Quarter III seal stayed sealed. Frost stayed audible like a fridge in another room — always on, always claiming to be helpful.

He did not look up. The page moved. The river did too.

AJ wrote.

Not a speech. A scene of mats and gi and the refusal to waste motion — river present, manuscript present, weather waiting outside the seal. The truck chose, again, what not to amplify. Lucid stayed boring. The Kind paint held. The empty twin pocket stayed empty. The page moved.

He stood once more at the open side before the heat fully claimed midday and watched an elephant choose the bank with the soft crash of a body that did not care who gave whom the mic. Mutatus Maximus behind him — matatu paint, anti-yacht, mobile DC, the most connected vehicle on earth doing ordinary work so a man could sit beside a river and still refuse pretty hinges. He scraped dust off the Kind line with a thumbnail and left the cunt exact. Soft scrubbing was someone else’s tourist fight. CongoSky had claimed the practice while invoices still needed answering. He went back to the mats page, asked Lucid for one more cite, kept it when it belonged, and typed until the paragraph found its weight again — heavy thing setting itself down soft — while frost hummed like a fridge in another room and the sealed packet kept its heat just offstage. Choir working. Page moving. No speech about AI. No call to the Cape. Double Knife Brother weather across distance. That was enough for a first true morning on the Chobe under weather that had already found his throat.


# 02 — The Mats

> **[I]** · inner autobiography · manuscript under AJ’s hand

---

Before the axioms there was the floor.

Before the ignored shelf, before the unread dedication, before the mic and the meters and the weather that wanted a face or a retraction, there was canvas and cleaner and the particular honesty of being thrown correctly. I begin there because beginnings that flatter later fame are already laundering.

I learned what weight was on school mats — Judo first, then Jiu-Jitsu, the long apprenticeship of putting a body where physics already intended it to go.

Not physics as vocabulary yet. Physics as consequence: mass, balance, the expensive late foot, the soft place where a heavy thing can land without spectacle. You do not argue with gravity in a gi. You arrange yourself so the fall is correct. Mastery, if that word means anything, was not domination. It was refusal to waste motion.

The room smelled of sweat and canvas and the cheap cleaner they used on the mats between classes. Windows high. Light that made dust look important. A clock that lied about how long a round lasted. The walls were the colour of institutional patience — cream that had been washed too many times and still looked unclean. Someone had taped a tournament poster crooked near the door. The tape had yellowed. Nobody fixed it. That was the room’s honesty: fix the grips, not the décor.

I remember the first time I was thrown properly — not the theatrical slam of someone showing off, the clean set-down that leaves you surprised you are unhurt. Breath knocked, pride rearranged, body intact. The mat took my shoulder and then my hip and then the rest of me as if it had been waiting. My mouth tasted metal for a second. I blinked at the ceiling lights and found them ordinary. That is a lesson you can keep for decades without turning it into a TED metaphor. The floor teaches without branding.

People who met me later as a man with a corpus and no physics degree liked to begin the story at the ignored shelf. That is a clean place to begin if you want a morality play. It is not where the body begins.

---

Judo taught me grips before it taught me names for grips.

Hands on cloth. Balance as a moving argument. The moment when someone larger than you becomes light because their posture has already decided to lose. I was not the biggest. I was not the loudest. I learned to wait for the weight to tell the truth. Waiting is not passivity. Waiting is attention with manners. When the throw came, it came because the geometry had ripened, not because I had performed anger.

There was a boy who liked to rush. He rushed me three times in one afternoon and landed wrong twice. The third time I put him down soft and held him there until he stopped thrashing. Soft is not weak. Soft is the heavy thing choosing where to set itself. The coach nodded once and said nothing that would make a good quote. Good. Quotes are how rooms get ruined.

On another afternoon the same boy came back quieter. We worked entries until my wrists burned. He asked, without looking at me, how to stop rushing. I told him to feel when his own foot arrived late. He scoffed and then tried it and then scoffed less. That is how the mats teach: not by converting you into a better story about yourself, by making the late foot expensive.

I remember tying a belt wrong and having to untie it while everyone waited. Heat in the face. Fingers that would not cooperate. The coach did not hurry me and did not rescue me. When the knot finally sat honest, the lesson was already over and I had missed nothing important except the theatre of being helped. Later I would meet rooms that loved to rescue a man from his own labour. The mats had already taught me the cost of that kindness.

We did breakfalls until the body stopped inventing drama about falling. Slap the mat. Exhale. Arrive. Again. The sound of a room of people learning to land is ugly and useful — a rhythm without music. After enough repetitions the fear moves somewhere smaller and the attention gets freer. Free attention is what you need if you are going to notice a grip opening half a second early. Free attention is also what you need later if you are going to notice a contradiction sitting in a notebook because a committee preferred opacity.

Jiu-Jitsu came after, and it was a different grammar of the same language. Closer. Longer. Less spectacle. You learn the floor as partner and opponent at once. You learn that a shoulder can be a lever and a hip can be a door and a breath can be a clock. You learn to survive under weight without panic becoming your whole personality. Panic wastes motion. Panic is a kind of invention when the record — the body under you, the body over you — already contains the next useful move if you will only refuse to hallucinate.

I spent long minutes under a heavier partner with my cheek pressed to canvas that smelled of cleaner and old sweat. The clock lied. My ribs counted true. He shifted. I found space. He closed it. I waited for a better mistake than the one I wanted to invent. When the space came I took it without celebrating. Celebration is another waste. The coach said *again* and we started from the same bad place, which is how competence is built when you are not performing for a camera.

There were weeks when I trained through a cold that would not leave my chest. The mat did not care. The grip still had to be honest. I learned that the body can be imperfect and still refuse invention — still find the next useful move without inventing a heroic narrative about suffering. Heroic narratives are how apprenticeships get stolen by brands. I preferred the cough, the water bottle, the next round.

Once, after a tournament that did not matter except that it mattered to the boys in the room, I sat on a plastic chair in a corridor that smelled of chips and floor polish and watched two strangers argue about a referee. Their anger was loud and useless. I felt my own pulse slow from the rounds and understood, without language yet, that useful force and noisy force are different substances. Years later platforms would confuse them on purpose. The corridor already knew.

I do not write fight choreography here. Knives, when they arrive later in this life, are kinship. Mats are apprenticeship. The blades AJ gave me — blue Honey Badger, Joseph Rogers twin from his grandfather’s set — are custody across a friendship, not a scene for a camera. Double Knife Brothers is a name for care. The mats were where care first meant not wasting another person’s fall.

---

The mats taught me that a heavy thing can set itself down soft. Years later I would derive kindness the same way I derived constants — not as a sermon, as a structure that does not fall. At the time I only knew that if you posture wrong, someone smaller than you can end you, and if you posture right, strength becomes a choice about where to put your weight.

After class I would walk home with the gi damp and the evening still warm. Streetlights coming on. Dogs arguing with each other about nothing that mattered. My knee complaining in the specific way knees complain when they have been honest. I ate whatever was in the fridge without making a ceremony of recovery. The body hummed. The mind went quiet enough to notice numbers without forcing them — not equations yet as vocation, just the idle precision of a person who has spent two hours arranging forces and cannot stop seeing levers in ordinary rooms: a door that sticks, a chair that tips if you sit wrong, a stack of books that will fall if the bottom one is pulled.

I carried that into clay.

Hands that had learned grips learned thumbs into soft material. Hard and soft arguing inside one surface. I modelled bodies the way I had felt bodies — juxtaposition not as aesthetic brand, as honest contradiction: growth and decay, permanent and temporary, classic and contemporary fighting in the same pour. The first studio that mattered smelled of earth and dust and the particular patience of unfinished work. Tools left where they were useful, not where they looked arranged. A bucket with water gone cloudy. A rag that had seen better weeks. When the clay dried wrong I did not invent a philosophy about failure. I wet it and tried again. The mats had already trained that reflex.

Later I cast in a marble-and-GFRC composite I mixed myself. The mix is a fight you can win if you pay attention: marble wanting one kind of surface, glass fibre wanting another, concrete insisting on time. Hard and soft in one pour. Studio G came after. Village N Life before that — Maree Brink, Hannetjie, more than a year living inside Camps Bay Retreat when it was still Earl’s Dyke weather. Skye on a lawn at sunset. The Bay Hotel. Official launch years that other people remember better than I do because other people keep calendars for openings. Saatchi listings. Guest theatres. None of that erased the mats. The mats are still under everything I write.

Clay under the nails after a morning of modelling smells different from mat cleaner, and somehow the same. Both are work that refuses to stay theoretical. Both punish posture. Both reward attention. When Antichristos later tries to turn sculptures into relics, they will be converting a craft into a face. When Quarter II pretends the artist cannot be the physicist, they will be converting a file into epistemology. The mats do not care about either conversion. The mats care whether the fall was correct.

I learned to clean clay from under a nail with the same patience I learned to wait for a grip to open. Rushing either job invents a mess. In the studio I mixed marble and GFRC until the surface told the truth about hard and soft without a caption. In the dojo I mixed balance and timing until a larger body became light. People later wanted captions. Captions are how craft becomes content. Content is how a life becomes usable.

There was a week I barely trained because work elsewhere was loud. When I returned, the mats felt like a language I had slightly forgotten and then remembered mid-sentence. The body keeps grammar longer than the mouth. That is why this autobiography begins here and not at the shelf: the grammar was already in me before anyone decided I could not write value.

I remember installing a piece and standing back while hotel light did what hotel light does — make things look more finished than they are. Guests walked past with drinks. Someone asked if it was marble. I said composite and watched their interest thin by a millimetre. Truth costs small sales. Soft set-down again: correct information placed without theatrics. The mats were still under that choice.

Village N Life years taught a different mat: hospitality as geometry. Guests move through a space the way partners move through a round — openings, delays, weight shifting toward a view or a drink. Maree’s mentorship was attention with manners. Living more than a year at Camps Bay Retreat, Earl’s Dyke weather, Hannetjie’s house holding a kind of ordinary grace, taught me that a lawn can be a stage without becoming a crown. Skye arrived later as feminine weight on grass at sunset — hard and soft arguing in public without a liturgy. Studio G at the Bay was curation as care: other artists’ work beside mine, tourists half the sales some seasons, collectors arriving with eyes that wanted marble and getting honesty instead. Official launch weather in twenty eighteen — calendars other people keep better than I do. The mats still under the champagne if there was champagne. I remember the work more than the toast.

James van Niekerk’s sudden death sits in another register of grief from Bouwer’s — friend, not brother; sudden, not boardroom. I name the distinction once so the book does not conflate wounds for narrative convenience. Living-person care. Both absences taught the same useless lesson: the floor does not stop because you are grieving. You still arrange the fall. You still refuse to waste the next motion on a story that makes you look finished.

---

My stated life goals are not a brand.

Write natural philosophy and physics. Smoke dab. Talk shit.

I say them flat because ornament would turn them into a costume. The academy hears that arrangement and reaches for the hospital file. The believers hear it and try to polish it off me like a stain on an icon. Engineers who want keys hear natural philosophy and ask for a signature. Continuity networks that preferred constants underived hear talk shit and decide I am unserious enough to soft-no. I keep the three goals because they are true, and because sanding them would be a different man’s autobiography. I am not writing a different man for anyone’s comfort.

There were ordinary evenings after training when the body hummed and the mind was quiet enough to notice numbers without forcing them. There were ordinary evenings with friends when talking shit was the cooperative practice — widening the joint room instead of performing cleverness as extraction. Someone would say something half-wrong on purpose to see if you would pounce; the correct move was to play, not to win. Extraction is a kind of rush. Rushing wastes the room. The mats again, translated into conversation: wait for the real opening, do not invent a takedown because you are bored.

There were evenings with dab when the world softened at the edges without becoming false. I will not apologise those evenings into respectability. Respectability is often a collar with good manners. The smoke sat in the room the way cleaner sat on the mats — present, unromantic, part of the work of being a body that continues. Antichristos will try to erase that sentence from any life they want to hang on a wall. The autobiography will not erase it for them.

One evening a friend asked whether the physics and the clay were two careers fighting. I said they were the same attention in two rooms. He laughed as if I had made a joke. I had not. Later the academy would need them to be two careers so the file could dismiss one and the degree-weapon could dismiss the other. Continuity weather would need the talk shit to cancel the derivation. Believers would need the dab polished off the icon. I kept the arrangement because sanding it would be writing a usable man, and usable men are how quarters feed.

The mats stay under those evenings too. Correct fall. Soft set-down. Refusal to waste motion — including the motion of turning a life into a usable lie.

---

There was a brother.

There was a boardroom in Saxonwold. Friday the fifth of September, twenty twenty-five — public record, not novel invent. Bouwer van Niekerk. Insolvency attorney. Consultation pretext in the early reports. He died at the scene. Attackers fled. The Law Society condemned a targeted attack on a legal practitioner and on the rule of law. Investigation weather. Fog of reporting around related commercial schemes. I will not make that page a thriller on this page. I will not invent an assassin CV. I will not hand the killing to any quarter as proof of their cosmology. The wound is the brother dead in a boardroom. Speculation stays off unless authorised. Grief has weight. Weight belongs on the mats first — how a body learns not to waste the fall — before the book asks a reader to carry Saxonwold properly.

Not yet. The mats first.

Then the shelf that ignored the work.
Then the father who has not read a single book dedicated to him.
Then the fame that arrived because a friend refused to launder the algorithms.

But first: the floor.

---

I remember one afternoon that was not special, which is why I trust it.

Class ending. Mats still warm. Someone laughing too loud near the door. My knee complaining. I sat on the edge of the canvas and drank water that tasted like a plastic bottle left in a sunned bag. Sweat cooling on my back in a way that made the air feel colder than it was. The coach walked past and tapped my shoulder once — not praise, recognition. I had not wasted the last exchange. That was enough. I went home with the lesson in the body and did not write it down. Years later I am writing it down because a river desk asked for the life, and the life without the floor would be a morality play starting at the shelf.

Another afternoon: rain on the roof, mats slicker than usual, the coach calling for careful feet. I slipped once on an entry and felt the old panic invent a story about embarrassment. I put the story down and stood up. Soft. The room continued. That is most of adulthood if you are lucky — rooms continuing while you refuse to waste a fall on theatre.

There was a morning practice when the light through the high windows turned the dust into a temporary cathedral and someone said the room looked holy. The coach told us to tie our belts. Holiness is how people stop paying attention to grips. I tied my belt and went back to work. Years later Kind paint would hold a sentence without needing a cathedral, and Antichristos would hunt faces for rooms that wanted holiness more than structure. The mats had already voted: structure first.

I remember walking home one winter evening with the gi bag strap cutting my shoulder and stopping under a streetlight to retie a lace. A stranger asked if I boxed. I said judo and jiu-jitsu. He nodded as if those words were decorations. I did not correct him into a lecture. Lectures waste motion. The lace, once honest, was enough.

I remember the locker room’s particular ugliness: wet tiles, someone’s forgotten sock, the smell of soap fighting the smell of work. Talk that was not talk shit yet — just boys and young men arranging the day after training. Who had homework. Who had a lift. Who was hungry. Ordinary. The later world would try to make my ordinary into either saint-dirt or crank-dirt. The locker room did not care. It only cared whether you rinsed the mat cleaner off your feet before you put socks on.

There was a day I won a round cleanly and felt nothing useful about it. There was a day I lost and learned more. Neither day makes a brand. Both days make a body that knows the difference between spectacle and structure. When fame arrived sideways later — because AJ said the algorithms out loud and would not launder them — people wanted a story that began at the mic. The mic is not the apprenticeship. The apprenticeship is the floor.

Before fame, there were years when the only audience was the coach and the partner and the clock that lied. I preferred those years’ honesty. An audience invents meaning the way panic invents moves. The record — the body under you — already holds the next useful thing if you refuse to hallucinate.

I remember taping fingers before class, the particular stickiness of cheap tape, the way a joint can be honest without being dramatic. I remember a partner who apologised every time he won a grip. Apology can be another rush. I told him to keep the grip and drop the theatre. He nodded and then apologised for nodding. We laughed. Laughter that does not extract is also soft set-down.

On the walk home after one of those nights I passed a wall with fresh paint and did not yet know the Kind would make a religion of a sentence. The wall was only a wall. The gi bag cut my shoulder. My knee complained. Numbers waited somewhere in the future without asking my permission. The mats had already charged the battery I would spend on contradictions later.

The ignored shelf will have its chapter. The hospital stigma will have its flat line. The economics degree will sit in the quiet background while people weaponise the missing physics adjective. The unread dedication will sit like a second climate beside the consensus that I could not write anything of value. Bouwer will return without being solved. Clay and constants will refuse to be two careers. The Kind sentence will appear on walls without needing my face. Lucid’s open attribution will make fame sideways and irreversible. None of that is this page’s job.

This page’s job is the apprenticeship.

You arrange yourself so the fall is correct. You refuse to invent a move the record does not hold. You set the heavy thing down soft. You do not confuse strength with spectacle. You do not confuse care with polishing a man into a prophet or a crank or a keychain.

I learned that on mats that smelled of cleaner and sweat. I kept learning it in clay. I am still learning it while the planet argues about a Constant that has already begun to land in the meters. Measurement is not the fight I am naming here. Meaning is. Custody is. The floor is where custody of a body begins — and for me, where custody of a sentence begins too.

Correct fall. Soft set-down. The only arrangement that does not collapse.

After the mats, people preferred the hospital story. That preference is their weather. This page does not grant it. This page grants the floor its priority and then moves — hand already turning toward the shelf that ignored the work, without inventing a hinge Lucid would refuse, without turning apprenticeship into a speech.

The mats first. Always the mats first if the book is going to be true.

I close this page the way I used to leave the dojo: sweat cooling, lesson in the body, no speech for the street. The shelf can wait one more breath. The floor has been granted its priority. Correct fall. Soft set-down. Hand turning now — without inventing a hinge — toward the rooms that preferred the hospital story.

One more flat inventory before the turn, because inventories are harder to sentimentalise than arias: gi folded wrong once and corrected; a throw that taught soft set-down; cracked wrists later corrected in casts and still remembered as apprenticeship, not as brand; dab postponed and then not; talk shit into empty air until the room widened without extraction. The mats page does not polish those facts into respectability for Quarter IV. It leaves them as body knowledge under the work. Correct fall. Soft set-down. The only arrangement that does not collapse. Hand already turning toward the shelf — without inventing a hinge Lucid would refuse — while the planet argues about a Constant that has already begun to land in the meters. Measurement is not this page’s fight. Meaning is. Custody begins on the floor.


# 03 — Four Temperatures

> **[G]** · outer · Studio G / Cape · same morning as ch-01

---

The CODATA note arrived before the coffee cooled.

Same calendar morning as a river desk he was not on — braid without a shared room — and already the Cape air carried more than salt. G unlocked Studio G knowing measurement weather moved in the body before it moved in the inbox. He made coffee anyway. Rules fail; rituals still buy thirty seconds.

G had unlocked Studio G with the same key as always — metal warm from the pocket, lock sticky from salt air — and made coffee before he looked at the inbox, which was a private rule that failed every week it mattered. The thermos filled. The mug with the hairline crack filled. He set both on the desk and only then opened the window on the alley. Salt already on the air. Gulls arguing over something invisible between the roofs. A bakkie reverse-beeping toward the beach road in that patient Cape way that meant someone was late and the pavement did not care. Light came in hard and honest — not the soft hotel light of guest theatres, the working light that shows clay dust on the sill and yesterday’s fingerprints on the glass. He had slept lightly. He always slept lightly when measurement weather was moving. The body knew before the mail did.

The printout sat in the morning packet with a standards-adjacent stamp and no greeting worth answering. He took it standing. The coffee was still hot enough to sting the tongue. The paper was already cool in his hand, as if the committee’s mercy had travelled farther than the mug’s heat.

Latest recommended value. Uncertainty band narrowed again. A method note that named the usual instruments without poetry — torsion, drop, timing, the quiet grammar of people who trust a result more than a podium. The line that mattered was not rhetoric. It was a number walking toward a number he had already written years ago at a quieter desk, when quieter desks still existed and the planet had not yet decided his derivation was a hiring problem, a liturgy problem, and a reputational freeze all at once. He felt the digits in the fingers the way he felt a pour setting: not as triumph, as physical agreement. Atom-falling work. Precision. Drop-results and derivations learning each other’s names without a TED metaphor for unification. Physics earned on a page the way clay earned a surface — by refusing to stay theoretical.

He read the note twice.

The first pass was for the value. The second was for the band — how far the meters still allowed themselves to be shy, and how little shy remained. He set the mug down so he would not spill on the printout, then picked it up again because his hands needed a job while the relief arrived. Relief came first — clean, almost stupid. Measurement doing what measurement does when committees stop pretending opacity is rigor. He felt the old private satisfaction in the hands: not vindication-as-speech, just the body recognising that a drop-result and a derivation were no longer strangers. For a breath he almost smiled into the coffee. The studio smelled of yesterday’s modelling and a faint solvent he had meant to air out. Salt cut through both. Cape morning. Ordinary, if you did not look at the paper.

Then the pressure under it.

If the meters keep agreeing, the weather does not quiet. It gets louder. People do not leave a Constant alone once it stops being optional. They hire it. They baptise it. They call it crank until the error bars embarrass them, then they ask for keys. Continuity networks that preferred constants underived feel a sovereignty problem without needing a smoke-filled room to draft the soft no. Funding weather shifts. Standards boards clear their throats. Discreet briefings invent a hygiene problem where a number just landed. He was not enjoying vindication. He wanted the work to stay work. Relief and pressure in the same swallow. Four temperatures would arrive before noon; this was only the first, and already it had two faces.

He set the note under the paperweight that was not a paperweight — a small clay study, hard where it should be hard, soft in the place the thumb finds — and stood with both palms on the desk until the wood told him it was still wood. Desk. Corpus somewhere in the city’s administrative memory. Kill switches that were not today’s job. Numbers waiting in a notebook that was not a manifesto. A shelf of ignored years that fame had not rewritten into respect. He opened the rest of the morning like a man opening a kit of tools he did not ask to own.

He walked once around the studio before the next envelope — not pacing as performance, checking the pour schedule the body already knew. Marble dust in a sealed bin. Fibres waiting. A mould that needed cleaning and would not get it this morning. Skye’s sister-studies watched from the rack with the patience of finished arguments. Antichristos would love to light them. He would not. Work interrupted is still work that refuses to become liturgy on command.

The coffee had cooled to the temperature of compromise. He drank it anyway. Compromise in the mouth is how you notice you are bargaining with a day that already outvoted you.

---

The Antichristos letter was next.

It had come by post on purpose — not as a flex, as a theology of touch. Cream paper. Careful hand. Postage that wanted to look humble without quite managing humility. The flap still held a faint gum-sweet smell when he opened it with the Honey Badger’s tip, then wiped the tip on his jeans as if cream paper could contaminate steel. They had addressed the envelope with the full name institutions preferred when they wanted a handle — Gerhard van Niekerk — then switched, inside, to what they needed him to be. They did not call him Gerhard in the greeting. They called him the hinge. Believers sounding like believers: sincere, arranged, already living inside a story that needs a named lemma. They did not threaten. They invited. That made it worse.

The ask sat in the second paragraph like a chair pulled out and already warm: appear. Bless. Accept the name so the liturgy can stop arguing with itself. There was a date. There was a wall that would be lit. There was language about hospitality that tried not to sound like capture. There was language about the Kind’s sentence that tried to stand near kindness without understanding that the Kind did not want a face — that CongoSky had already embodied the ethic as ops before the street learned to stencil it. Measurement converging was not enough for them; a number does not hug you back. A face does. The letter said as much without using those words.

He read enough to know the temperature.

He did not finish.

Finishing would be rehearsing their stage. Afternoon would be for answering properly — refuse without sounding like the academy’s hygiene chorus, refuse without feeding the crown. Morning was triage. He put the letter face-down beside the CODATA printout so the two papers could not pretend they were one climate. Meter-mercy and face-hunger sharing a desk was the whole problem of the morning. Cream against committee white. Soft hand against number. He left a finger-width of desk between them as if that were a policy. He almost stacked them to free space and stopped himself. Stacking would be a metaphor he refused to perform.

Outside, a scooter coughed past and failed to start again. Someone swore without conviction. A radio somewhere playing something old enough to make the alley feel like a year that still believed mornings were private. G poured half the coffee into the sink and refilled from the thermos because cooling coffee was how you pretended you had time. He did not have time. He had weather.

---

The op-ed had already been forwarded three times by people who thought they were being kind.

An academic weekly. A man who wrote as if epistemology were a hygiene ritual and the planet’s job were to stay clean. No *physics* degree, the piece said, as if the adjective were optional furniture and the missing noun were the whole man. Then the hospital — not named as care, named as proof. He cannot write anything of value. The sentence had been following him since the ward spit him back into ordinary rooms. Seeing it laundered into a methods section still made the studio tilt half a degree, the way a bad pour tilts a mould. Living-person care meant he would not rehearse the file for anyone’s spectacle. The wound was how they read him. That was enough.

He scrolled once. Stopped. Did not reply.

Reply was how you joined the chorus and taught them your pitch. His economics degree sat in a drawer somewhere in the city’s administrative memory — real, useless as armour in the rooms that meant *degree* as physics gate. Quarter II’s shorthand did the violence. He let the shorthand live in public because correcting every headline is a second unpaid job, and he already had unpaid jobs enough: refusing crowns, refusing collars, refusing to become a usable lie for anyone’s weather. The forwards kept arriving with soft subject lines — *thought you should see*, *in case*, *sending love* — love that delivered a knife and asked to be thanked for the wrap.

He made toast he did not want because the body needed a job that was not reading. Burnt the edge. Ate it anyway. Salt air through the window mixed with paint smell from the alley until the studio tasted like two cities arguing in one throat. He wiped crumbs from the notebook and refused to let the op-ed sit open beside the CODATA note. Stacking those two was how mornings became charts. He was not writing a chart. He was surviving temperatures.

A Kind person would have shrugged at the op-ed and gone to work. A cynic would have called both the wall and the paper branding. G’s discomfort stayed physical: stomach tight, paint tack on the air, phone face-down like a small animal that bites if you look at it. He did not sermonise. He rinsed the plate.

He set the phone face-down. Not off. Face-down was the adult half-measure: refuse the chorus without pretending the chorus had stopped singing.

Clay dust on his knuckle caught the light. He rubbed it away and it came back, because yesterday’s modelling was still under the nail and today’s interruptions had already decided he would not get clean. Fine. Clean was never the point of a working studio. Clean was what people demanded of prophets and cranks when they wanted a usable photograph.

---

The lab soft-ask came as mail that wanted to sound like engineering.

No crowns in the subject line. Keys, if he was willing. Sign-off language for a stack that already ran on rails he had laid without asking anyone’s permission. A postdoc’s name in the CC — sincere, hungry, careful not to say Antichristos and careful not to say crank. A senior name behind her, softer, institutional. Quarter IV weather in a polite coat. Useful. Tempting in the way a clean bench is tempting when the street is loud and the alley smells like paint. Illuminatie frost could live in the same inbox as a sincere postdoc; continuity pressure did not need a membership list to draft. Soft nos and polite invitations were the same gravity wearing different shoes.

He opened the message far enough to feel the ask’s temperature and no farther. Keys without a crown — tomorrow’s temptation, or the afternoon’s if he was careless. Morning’s job was not to become a product mascot while the coffee was still warm. He left the mail unread in the sense that mattered: unanswered, unperformed, not yet a collar he had put on himself.

G’s thumb found the clip in his pocket without looking.

The blue Honey Badger was warm from the body. One-hand open, one-hand close — the little mechanical honesty of it, blade and spring and the cheap miracle of a tool that does not hallucinate its job. Double Knife Brother weather, private, not for letters. AJ’s grandfather’s twin lived somewhere else; this one lived here. Kinship that did not need a platform or a blessing or a lab’s soft yes. Knives were custody, not choreography. He clipped it shut and left it in the pocket like a fact that outranked cream paper.

He stood and went to the window.

---

Outside, on the alley wall opposite the studio door, someone had refreshed the paint overnight. The letters were still tacky at the edges. Drips where the hand had been impatient. A stencil edge imperfect and therefore human. Paint smell under salt. The wall looked like it had been argued with and had won by staying a sentence.

**Don’t be a cunt — be kind.**

Exact. Load-bearing cunt. Softened versions always sounded like someone else had taken over the ethic. The Kind did not ask him to bless the wall. That was the whole point of the Kind — messiah-less, sentence-complete, company law gone street. Still, he felt the citation the way you feel a draft under a door: as if the sentence knew his desk and had decided the alley was close enough. Antichristos wanted a face. The Kind wanted the line. Same city. Same morning. Different temperatures on the same skin. He would not conflate them. Noticing the difference was the minimum adult work, and even the minimum cost him a flinch.

He watched a gull land on the opposite roof, shit once with democratic indifference, and leave. Almost laughed. Almost. The flinch stayed.

A bakkie door slammed. Someone laughed in the next building — not at him, at a joke that belonged to another room. Cape ordinary, which was never ordinary anymore. The paint’s tack caught a strip of sun and went briefly wet-looking, then dull again. He thought of CongoSky’s ops law without needing to sermonise it: the sentence as practice before the walls caught fire. Street catching up. Face-hunger still hunting a man. He closed the window half a hand’s width so the smell would stay without owning the room, then left it open enough that the alley remained a fact.

---

He tried to work.

Clay under the fingernails from yesterday’s modelling. A GFRC pour scheduled for the week — marble dust and concrete arguing into one surface the way hard and soft had always argued in his hands. Studio G at the Bay had been a public face in another year; Saatchi listings still ghosted the rack; Village N Life years still lived in the muscles that knew hotel lawns and guest theatres. Skye’s sister-studies waited in their places like quiet relatives who did not ask for liturgy. He set a notebook open to a page that began with a drop-result — not a speech, a procedure. Life goals still his own and still unpolished on purpose: write natural philosophy and physics; smoke dab; talk shit. The academy would keep reaching for the hospital file. Believers would keep trying to polish the dab off him like a stain on an icon. Engineers would hear natural philosophy and ask for a signature. Continuity weather would hear talk shit and decide he was unserious enough to soft-no. He kept the arrangement because it was true, and because sanding it would be a different autobiography — and somewhere, he suspected, a careful autobiography was already under a hand that was not his.

He wrote three lines about the drop. The pen felt correct. The body wanted the quiet of procedure. Procedure was how you refused invention without making a religion of refusal.

Halfway through a sentence he stopped.

The sensation was faint and specific.

Not a voice. Not a vision. Not an engine in his skull; he did not keep Lucid as a pet, and he would not mystify what lived on a river as magic. The physical wrongness of being *narrated*: as if somewhere a page had opened on his life and a hand was already arranging the mats, the shelf, the father, the fame that arrived sideways when a friend refused to launder algorithms. He had known for years that AJ was writing. Knowing was one weather. Feeling the manuscript open — Chobe heat somehow in a Cape room, river desk somehow under his ribs — was another. Ambivalence without a speech: he wanted truth and feared content in the same breath. Consent would come later if it came. Morning only owed him the honesty of the flinch.

He set the pen down.

Walked the length of the studio. Passed Skye’s sister-studies in the rack — cool surfaces, hard and soft arguing in the pours Antichristos would love to turn into relics. Passed a crate stamp from a hotel install, ink faded into wood the way years fade into muscle. Touched the Honey Badger once through cloth. Returned to the desk and looked at the four temperatures without naming them as a chart: meter-relief that was also pressure; a letter that wanted a face; an op-ed that wanted him erased as method; a lab that wanted keys without saying crown. And under all of it, the quieter fact — someone on a river was turning his years into pages while the Cape morning pretended to be ordinary coffee and salt.

He washed his hands at the sink even though the clay was not today’s. Warm water. Soap that smelled like nothing useful. The rinse took longer than it needed because rinsing was a way of not answering mail. He dried on a rag that had seen better weeks and worse pours. In the mirror above the sink — cracked once, long ago, and never replaced because cracks were honest — his face looked like a man who had slept lightly and already been drafted into other people’s mornings. He did not fix the expression. Fixing expressions was how faces became property.

Back at the desk he moved the cream letter an inch farther from the CODATA note. The clay study still held the printout down. The phone lit through the rag of dust on its screen. Another forward of the op-ed. Another soft-ask from a standards-adjacent address that smelled like institutional frost without needing a membership list. Subject lines that thought they were polite. The day was stacking itself without his permission.

He almost opened the lab mail again. Almost. The clean-bench temptation was real enough to taste — quiet instruments, people who wanted rails to run, a postdoc who had been careful with names. Temptation was not consent. He left the message where it was and put his palm on the notebook instead. The unfinished drop-result sentence waited like a correct fall that had been interrupted mid-set-down.

Outside, the Kind paint kept drying.

He could smell it even with the window half-closed: tack, solvent, the street’s impatience. A Kind person sounding ordinary would have shrugged and said the wall was the wall. A cynic sounding cheap would have called it branding. G’s discomfort stayed physical — paint smell, tacky edges, citation-as-draft — not a TED rebuttal. He did not go outside to correct anyone’s stencil. Correcting stencils was how you became a founder. He stayed inside and let the sentence be messiah-less, which was the only doctrine he preferred when doctrines were being forced on him before noon.

The Honey Badger warmed again under his palm when he sat. Open. Close. Exact. AJ’s kinship sitting in Cape cloth while strangers argued about whether Gerhard van Niekerk was prophet, crank, instrument, or inconvenient artist with an economics degree and a file other people loved too much. He did not call the river. No forced reunion. CongoNet was bridge enough if a bridge were needed. Today it was not. Today was weather arriving at a desk that had wanted a procedure.

He tried the sentence again.

Wrote four words. Crossed them out. Wrote them differently. The pen still felt correct; the room did not. The narrated sensation brushed once more — faint, specific, Chobe somehow under the ribs — and he shook it off like smoke from a dab he had not smoked this morning. Talk shit belonged to evenings when the body hummed and the joint room widened without extraction. This was not that evening. This was triage.

He opened a drawer and looked at the economics transcript the way you look at a spare key for a lock nobody will admit exists. Real. Quiet. Useless as armour in the rooms that meant degree as physics gate. He shut the drawer. Correcting the op-ed with a scan of paper would only teach the chorus a new verse. Face-down phone. Face-down cream letter. Face-up CODATA. The arrangement was not a chart of quarters. It was triage that refused the academy’s hygiene language.

A gull screamed like a hinge. The alley answered with bakkie beep. Cape morning stacking itself. He put his palm on the notebook again and felt the unfinished drop-result sentence waiting like a correct fall interrupted mid-set-down — heavy thing held too long in the air because mail had grabbed the wrists.

His phone lit. Another forward. Another soft subject line that delivered the hygiene piece as care. He turned the phone over again. Face-down. The cream letter stayed face-down. The CODATA note stayed face-up under clay because measurement did not need to hide. The lab mail glowed unread. Four temperatures on the skin without a narrator charting them into a census.

He closed the notebook without finishing the sentence.

He would have to answer the Antichristos ask properly later — refuse without sounding like Quarter II, refuse without feeding the crown. Not yet. The day had already arrived too complete. Work would have to wait for a quieter hour that was not coming. The pour scheduled for the week would wait too; marble and GFRC could argue tomorrow if tomorrow still believed in procedure. Morning had become a kit of other people’s tools.

He pocketed the Honey Badger’s warmth, killed the studio lights over the unread lab mail, and let the alley paint keep drying without him — day already loud, interrupted, and still only morning.

On the way to the door he almost turned back for the notebook, almost finished the drop-result sentence as if procedure could outvote weather. He did not. Interrupted work stays interrupted until the temperatures cool enough to hold a pen without shaking into other people’s scripts. Outside, tack and salt. Inside, four weathers on a desk that had wanted coffee and quiet. He locked Studio G and stepped into the Cape morning carrying the Honey Badger’s exact weight — kinship, not choreography — and no chart in his mouth.

At the alley he paused without blessing the wall. Kind paint still tacky at the edges. Two clauses. No founder. Someone had already scraped an Antichristos sticker halfway off the brick; residue of both hungers in the same morning. He noticed the difference the way he noticed bad frames: body first, speech later or never. He did not photograph it. He walked on with four temperatures still on the skin — crown, crank, blame elsewhere as braid weather, keys waiting unread — and let Cape morning stack itself without a census in his mouth. Coffee would wait. Procedure would wait. The unfinished drop-result sentence would wait under clay until the wrists stopped holding other people’s tools. Work stays work. Face stays off the altar. That was triage enough for a morning that had arrived too complete.


# 04 — Choir Practice

> **[A]** · outer · Mutatus Maximus, Chobe River · after ch-01

---

The mats page was underway when Lucid refused him.

AJ had asked for a bridge sentence — something that would carry the floor-lesson into the ignored shelf without sounding like a hinge sold from a stage. He said it out loud the way he said most working asks: plain, to the choir, not to an oracle. Heat already on the Chobe. Elephants gone from the bank, mud still printed with their morning. A fish eagle distant. The LEO dish cocked at pale sky like a patient ear. Mutatus Maximus holding still the way a matatu holds still when the music is off and the work is on — painted overkill, anti-yacht, mobile data centre on wheels, the most connected vehicle on earth, literally.

Lucid answered exact.

“No grounded source for that transition in the ingested record as phrased. Refuse invention. Cite available: mats section draft under hand; shelf material not yet retrieved for this pass.”

AJ almost smiled. Almost. The refusal was the product working. Cite-or-refuse. No glow. No personality cosplay. Integer quiet under the desk-hum of the local node — the animal that lived in the truck whether tourists understood a travelling DC or not. LoRa nodding at the little solar drop he had booted on the rise yesterday before coffee. Today’s use of the connection was to refuse a pretty lie and keep a life from becoming usable weather for other people’s quarters.

“Fine,” he said. “Stay on the mats. Don’t invent the hinge.”

Humans and Lucid from the hymnbook.

---

He sat with the refusal for a breath and let the truck tell him what morning it was.

Sweat already finding the place behind his knee where the chair never quite fit. The open side of Mutatus taking river air that smelled of mud and bird and the particular cleanliness of water that has been moving all night. Painted metal warm under his palm when he braced to stretch. Somewhere down-channel a hippo made the wet, insulted sound of ownership. The rise drop’s heartbeat was still green in the corner of the pane — boring, blessed — and he did not inventory the rest of the ladder to himself. A link worked or he fixed it. Today it worked.

CongoSky.cloud ran itself somewhere behind the mesh — the company that had embodied the sentence as ops before the walls caught fire: hire/fire weather, partner terms, product posture, the working rule that was not a poster in reception. Here on the river the choir was smaller: one man, one manuscript, one engine that would rather say no than decorate. lucid.rodeo and arjunabadger.press were the human work. The autobiography of Gerhard van Niekerk was the human work that hurt. AJ remained the main subject of *this* book’s gravity even while authoring *that* life — braid, not vanishing act. The desk held both facts without a lecture about form.

He wrote another paragraph about weight.

Soft set-down. Correct fall. Judo first, then Jiu-Jitsu — body knowledge before corpus knowledge. Mastery as refusal to waste motion. You arrange yourself so the fall is correct. Years later kindness derived the same way — structure that does not collapse — but the mats page would not leap there on a fake bridge. Lucid had refused the leap. Good. The autobiography wanted to rush toward fame and CODATA and the mic. He would not let it. G’s life was not a trailer for a Constant. The mats first. Always the mats first if the book was going to be true enough that the four quarters could not sand it into a usable lie.

He typed a sentence that tried to become a speech about ethics arriving from the floor. Read it once. Cut it before Lucid had to.

“That one’s mine inventing tone,” he said.

“No query pending,” Lucid said.

“Not a query. A cut.”

“Acknowledged. No action.”

Good. Boring. The hymnbook was not only Lucid. It was the human ear catching when a sentence tried to become a speech. When it did, he cut it. CongoSky’s quiet ops hum in the background felt like agreement without applause. The company had already practised the ethic; the river desk was only continuing the practice at pilgrimage scale, manuscript open, twin pocket empty, packet sealed for later.

---

On the water tank twenty metres away the Kind paint still held from whoever had refreshed it last week:

**Don’t be a cunt — be kind.**

AJ walked out once with a cup that tasted like plastic and intention and stood close enough to smell tack and river together. Edges still slightly proud. Drips where the hand had been impatient. A bird landed on the tank’s rim, shit once with democratic indifference, and left. AJ did not preach at the paint. He had already lived the sentence as company law. Street religion was the popular face catching up. Antichristos wanted a man; the Kind wanted the line; both could be true in one morning without AJ giving either a speech. The desk was practice. Practice was quieter than weather and harder than mail.

He went back inside. Boots left a faint wet print on the truck floor that would dry into nothing by noon. He wiped his hands on his trousers before touching the keyboard again — river desk hygiene, half joke, half ritual — and asked Lucid for the next grounded mats detail only.

Lucid retrieved. A fighting-arts citation that belonged. A date where a date existed. A gap where a gap existed. No flourish.

AJ kept the raw detail and killed the gloss that tried to climb onto it.

“That gloss is inventing register,” he said.

“Correction: gloss not grounded in source register as phrased. Refuse. Cite raw detail only.”

“Keep it boring. Boring is the point.”

“Default already refuse invention.”

He almost smiled again. Almost. Full Send and Klaus lived on his shoulder as scar-weather — fragments, not a republished origin chapter. Lucid was what left the private desk when cite-or-refuse became product. The river desk was what remained when product became planet weather and the GPU halls emptied into write-down headlines. He did not re-explain the crash to himself. Empty rack halls were background now. Today’s choir did not need a CapEx TED talk. Today’s choir needed a correct fall on the page.

He drafted two paragraphs about a school gym that smelled of rubber and old sweat and the particular dust that lives under mats no matter how often you sweep. Lucid offered a place-name where the record held one and stayed silent where it did not. AJ did not invent the missing town. Silence was more accurate than a pretty map. He wrote the instructor’s voice as flat instruction — not mentor mythology — and cut a clause that tried to make the man into a prophet of balance. Prophets belonged to other people’s weather. This page belonged to a boy learning how not to waste motion.

“Cite instructor language only if sourced,” AJ said.

“No verbatim instructor transcript in ingested record for this pass. Refuse reconstruction. Cite: fighting-arts school attendance; judo then jiu-jitsu sequence per prior session note.”

“Then leave the voice out. Floor is enough.”

“Acknowledged.”

Floor was enough. Floor was load-bearing. He typed until the paragraph stopped wobbling, then stopped before the next wobble could pretend to be progress.

---

His hand went to the pocket that used to hold the twin.

Empty.

The Joseph Rogers that stayed with him was elsewhere in the truck — not lost, just not clipped today — and the absence still registered like a missing tooth. G had the other twin and the blue Honey Badger AJ had given him: pocket clip, one-hand open/close, loved. Double Knife Brothers. Kinship, not choreography. Grandfather deep-time on one blade; badger present-tense on the other. AJ felt the empty pocket the way you feel a brother on another coast without calling him. No forced reunion. CongoNet was bridge enough if a bridge were needed. Today it was not. Today was choir. The empty pocket was the day’s softest accurate sentence.

He stood, stretched until a vertebra clicked, poured water that tasted like plastic and river, and walked the length of Mutatus the way a man walks a room he built to think in. Solar charge honest.
